#2155. Gary Lineker revealed how Sheffield Wednesday icon shaped Spurs decision

Gary Lineker has a Sheffield Wednesday legend to thank for shaping a significant part of his playing career and identity.

The now 65-year-old first came through with Leicester City in the late 1970s before playing for Everton and Barcelona.

The striker came close to breaking the record for goals scored for the England men’s national team, finding the back of the net on 48 occasions from 80 caps.

And in 1989, he returned to English football with a move to Tottenham Hotspur, and he credited a surprising figure for the decision to sign for the London outfit.

Speaking on the Match of the Day Top 10 podcast in 2021, the former BBC presenter revealed that getting the opportunity to play with Chris Waddle was a major factor in his decision to sign for Spurs.

However, he claimed that it was only after he’d agreed to the deal that his agent informed him that the former Sheffield Wednesday star had already signed for Marseille in a big-money move.

“He didn't look like a footballer, but I loved playing with him,” said Lineker, via the BBC.

Ali Al-Hamadi has reflected on his start to life at Sheffield Wednesday on social media, with his team-mate Barry Bannan reacting to the post.

“He was one of the main reasons I signed for Spurs when I left Barcelona.

“I did the deal and I was on holiday in the Caribbean and I got a phone call from my agent saying that he'd been sold to Marseille for £15 million.

“It was like someone just snatching 15 goals a season out of my pocket.

“But he is loved in Marseille, he's a proper cult hero there, he's one of their most popular players in their history.

“And he was the only person who could share a room with Gazza!”

Lineker still went through with the move to White Hart Lane, where he spent three years before departing English football again for a move to Japan with Nagoya Grampus.

During his time with Spurs, the forward scored 67 times from 105 appearances, winning the FA Cup in the process.

Lineker cited Waddle as a major inspiration for his decision to sign for Spurs, but perhaps it would’ve gone differently had he known about his move to Marseille.

The 65-year-old revealed in 2015 that he also had the option to join Manchester United in 1989, who were then managed by Alex Ferguson.

The Scot wanted to partner Lineker with Mark Hughes, with the club targeting a first league title since 1967.

Of course, the Red Devils went on to become a juggernaut in the 1990s, and the former England star must wonder what could’ve been had it not been for Waddle.

It was only after his time at Marseille that Waddle cemented himself as an icon at Sheffield Wednesday.

After three years in France, the playmaker made the switch to Hillsborough ahead of the first ever Premier League season in 1992.

How good was Chris Waddle for Sheffield Wednesday? Comment your opinion below

The now 65-year-old spent four seasons with the Owls, where he made 109 league appearances, contributing 10 goals and 22 assists, with the club finishing seventh, seventh, 13th and 15th in the table.

Even by the standards of someone in the latter stages of his career, Waddle’s performances for the Yorkshire outfit remained quite impressive, and he had a strong final year at Hillsborough, scoring two and assisting six in the Premier League at the age of 34.

While Lineker was unable to enjoy a spell at Spurs with Waddle as his teammate, the pair did, of course, feature together at international level.

In particular, the 1990 World Cup in Italy was a highlight of their time together, with the former Sheffield Wednesday player enjoying a strong tournament that ultimately ended up in disappointment in the penalty shootout in the semi-finals.

That tournament especially highlighted to Spurs fans what could’ve been with Lineker and Waddle working together, as it was England’s best ever showing in the competition on foreign soil until this year’s edition in North America.

#2153. Melvine Malard becomes Chelsea’s leading scorer as FOUR-goal start strengthens her claim to the No.9 role

Graduate Sports Journalist with a love for football.

Melvine Malard’s Chelsea goals are beginning to provide a persuasive answer to the club’s centre-forward question.

Her opener in Sunday’s 3-0 victory over West Ham took the summer signing to four competitive goals, making her Chelsea’s leading scorer this season.

That total comprises two goals in Champions League qualifying and two in the Women’s Super League.

The circumstances make those contributions particularly useful. Across three scoring appearances, Malard has broken a deadlock, restored a lead and helped Chelsea put difficult matches beyond their opponents.

Three of those goals arrived while Chelsea were level or protecting a one-goal advantage.

That gives the early return significance beyond its size. Malard has repeatedly contributed while there was still a contest to resolve.

Sunday provided the latest example. Chelsea had plenty of possession but needed somebody to turn an opening into a lead.

Turn and finish 💥Melvine Malard finishes for @ChelseaFCW 🔵#BarclaysWSL pic.twitter.com/eicBNrYMYk

According to Chelsea’s official match report, the Blues reached the half-hour mark without a shot on target despite having 64 per cent possession.

Sjoeke Nüsken created the breakthrough by winning possession and finding Malard.

The striker received the ball with her back to goal, held her position, turned and finished into the bottom corner.

It was an important demonstration of what she can offer centrally. Chelsea could find her under pressure and still get a shot away, rather than needing to release her into open space.

Nüsken’s contribution deserves equal recognition. Her pressing created the opportunity that Malard converted.

The sequence offers Bompastor something useful to build on: winning possession higher up the pitch and finding a forward capable of finishing before the opposition recover.

Malard’s first three Chelsea goals came as a substitute.

Her two-goal competitive debut against Real Sociedad immediately demonstrated that capacity to change a match.

Nüsken released her for the goal that restored Chelsea’s lead at 3-2. Ellie Carpenter subsequently supplied her second, extending the advantage to three goals.

The official account of that European victory records two different routes to goal: a run beyond the defence and a finish from a delivery across the box.

Against Birmingham, her alertness produced another valuable contribution. Malard punished hesitation over a backpass to score Chelsea’s second, eight minutes after coming on, as detailed in the club’s match report.

Those appearances give Bompastor flexibility. Malard has already influenced matches when introduced against tiring opponents, while West Ham supplied a scoring return from the starting role.

Chelsea signed Malard from Manchester United on a four-year contract, bringing her back together with a coach who first identified her potential at 14.

Bompastor later coached her in Lyon’s senior side. Their relationship therefore predates this summer’s recruitment by years.

Speaking when Chelsea announced her signing, Malard said:

“My objective is to become better every single day and to win trophies.”

Her next task is sustaining this contribution across a longer run of matches.

Competition remains productive. Aggie Beever-Jones replaced her at West Ham and supplied Maika Hamano’s late goal, giving Bompastor another reason to value both forwards.

Malard’s strongest argument is the range of situations she has already influenced. She has attacked space, finished crosses, punished hesitation and scored with a defender close behind her.

Four goals offer an encouraging early return. The variety behind them gives Chelsea something substantial to develop.

#2152. Burnley must sanction emotional Newcastle switch to fix key problem area

Burnley have endured a nightmare beginning to the 2026/27 Championship campaign following relegation from the Premier League, as the January transfer window looks increasingly important for the Clarets to get right in each passing week.

Burnley were relegated with a whimper from the Premier League last season, but heading back into the Championship, Clarets fans would have naturally been optimistic about their chances, having been automatically promoted the three previous times they found themselves in the division.

However, finding a replacement for Scott Parker proved to be difficult, and in truth, drawn out for way too long, as Belgian head coach Nicky Hayen was handed a poison chalice upon his arrival at Turf Moor.

Hayen won just one of his 12 league games in charge in Lancashire, as well as being knocked out of the League Cup at the hands of League One Bradford City, as incoming Swiss boss Fabio Celestini will be hoping to be given until January to make some much-needed changes to Burnley ranks.

Following his swift appointment, Celestini will have had time to work with his squad in training and identify key areas of improvement, which, in the Clarets’ case right now, is all over the pitch.

However, some positions are more problematic than others, as the options between the sticks right now at Turf Moor aren’t wholly convincing.

Max Weiss started the campaign between the sticks, but after a few high-profile errors, the German was dropped in favour of French goalkeeper Gregoire Coudert, who joined from Brest in the summer.

Since replacing Weiss between the sticks, Coudert has not covered himself in much glory either, shipping four away to Lincoln City and being at fault for Queen’s Park Rangers’ equaliser in their 1-1 draw at Loftus Road, as a highly recognisable name ought to be a priority this January to fix this evident issue.

If the tide doesn’t turn at Burnley sooner rather than later, the Clarets are at risk of following in the footsteps of the likes of Sunderland, Luton Town, and Leicester City to suffer double relegations from the Premier League to League One in consecutive years.

A solid pair of hands between the sticks can be a game-changer, as Celestini must look to bring out-of-favour Newcastle United shot-stopper Nick Pope back to the club in January.

Pope, 34, worked his way up the Football League with the likes of Bury Town, Harrow Borough, Welling United, Charlton Athletic, Cambridge United, Aldershot Town, York City, and Bury before earning a move to Burnley in the summer of 2016, initially acting as deputy to Tom Heaton under Sean Dyche.

155 appearances across all competitions later, Pope had announced himself as one of the finest shot-stoppers in the country, earning ten caps for England during Jordan Pickford’s dominant era, and eventually joining Newcastle United in the summer of 2022 for around £10 million following Burnley’s relegation.

At 34, Pope found himself battling with Southampton outcast Aaron Ramsdale for the number one spot at St James’ Park last season under Eddie Howe, and now is firmly behind Lukáš Horníček in the pecking order.

With Ewen Jaouen and Mark Gillespie also vying for minutes under Mathias Jaissle, Pope’s Newcastle career appears to be coming to a quiet end, as the former England international ought to push for a return to Turf Moor this winter.

Despite his age, few can deny his outstanding shot-stopping ability, and right now, you’d surely do well to find a Burnley supporter who doesn’t believe Pope would walk straight into their side right now in the Championship.

Burnley need a hero, and someone to increase the mood around the place, and given Pope’s situation at Newcastle, he could be the perfect fit.

The only obvious stumbling block is likely his wages, which may be too steep for the Clarets to match, though they would be remiss not to try, as Celestini has one obvious goal heading into January.

Burnley are in desperate need of an uptick in form sooner rather than later; otherwise, they risk being cut adrift from the rest of the pack.

The January window will provide Celestini with the chance to make some much-needed changes to his squad, and it would be a surprise if a new goalkeeper wasn’t high on his list.

The upcoming window could be Burnley’s last roll of the dice to secure survival, as the Clarets cannot afford to let it pass them by.

#2151. Danny Drinkwater on Chelsea move he ‘could never turn down’

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

The Danny Drinkwater Chelsea story still carries a sting. Five years at Stamford Bridge gave him plenty of time to think about how it all turned out, and his own verdict is not a happy one.

Drinkwater opened up in a Truth or Padel appearance alongside David Ornstein. He said the move was one he could never have turned down, even with the benefit of hindsight. “No, I don’t regret moving. I regret, in hindsight, how it’s turned out. I think I could never turn it down,” he said.

Strip away the headlines and the numbers alone tell an uncomfortable story. Drinkwater spent five years on Chelsea’s books. In that time he made just 23 appearances, according to the original interview report.

Do the maths and it barely comes to five games a year. That is a thin return for a central midfielder signed to compete for a first-team place. It is also the kind of record that rarely sits well at a club of Chelsea’s size and ambition.

Chelsea’s best attacking spells over a similar stretch of seasons look far more productive, as our striker rankings show. Set next to those numbers, Drinkwater’s five quiet years stand out even more starkly.

Five years. Twenty three games. These numbers alone explains why this transfer still gets talked about by Drinkwater, long after the headlines faded.

The numbers only tell part of the story. A string of incidents turned a quiet, underwhelming spell into something far more pointed.

Drinkwater had a training-ground bust-up while out on loan. He was also involved in an on-pitch brawl with Chelsea’s own under-23s side. Flashpoints between a first-team player and a development squad are rare at any club, let alone one with Chelsea’s standards.

Taken alone, either incident might have passed as a bad day. Taken together, it became harder to see Drinkwater simply as an unlucky squad player who could not catch a break.

The most serious moment came during his time at Aston Villa. Drinkwater headbutted team-mate Jota and was fined two weeks’ wages for it. He says the consequences at Chelsea did not stop there.

“They didn’t formally ban me, but I got removed from the first team building after the incident at Villa,” Drinkwater said.

One off-field incident, at another club, pushed him out of the manager’s day-to-day plans back home. No formal punishment at Chelsea followed. The effect was much the same.

Chelsea fans saw a similar breakdown of trust play out when Graham Potter struggled to win over the dressing room during another difficult Chelsea transition. Trust can break down fast once results or behaviour turn against the person at the centre of it.

Drinkwater’s comments matter beyond his own career. They are a reminder that saying yes to a big move does not guarantee it will work once a player walks through the door.

That tension has not gone away. Chelsea’s current transfer business, covered in our transfer watch, shows the club still weighing big calls on players whose futures depend on more than form alone.

Squad management under Xabi Alonso, assessed in our bomb squad verdict, raises a familiar issue. Players who fall outside a manager’s plans rarely find a quick way back in, whether the exile is formal or not.

Drinkwater never found his way back into Chelsea’s plans after the Villa incident. His account of the Danny Drinkwater Chelsea years stands as a blunt warning. A move that looks impossible to refuse can still become one of the hardest spells of a career.

#2150. Keira Walsh adds another Chelsea weapon as former Arsenal target scores THIRD goal of the season

Graduate Sports Journalist with a love for football.

Keira Walsh is giving Chelsea opponents another decision to make: protect the penalty area or step out to stop her shooting.

Her goal in Sunday’s 3-0 victory at West Ham took her to three for the season across all competitions. Each has come from outside the box.

For a player whose passing helps organise Chelsea’s attacks, that threat adds another way to influence matches. Recent performances show her contributing both to the construction of chances and their finish.

Against Sociedad, Walsh struck from distance after a corner, helping Chelsea complete their passage into the Champions League’s league phase.

The club’s account of that qualifying victory describes a tight encounter in which clear opportunities were limited.

At Manchester United, Lucy Bronze found her near the edge of the box and Walsh drove her finish into the bottom corner.

She also supplied Lauren James’ goal that afternoon, as Chelsea’s match report records. It was a useful illustration of the choices she offers: attempt the finish herself or release somebody closer to goal.

West Ham provided another example of that balance.

Walsh had already struck the post before Alyssa Thompson carried the ball in from the left and found Wieke Kaptein. Kaptein moved it on to Walsh, whose finish gave Chelsea breathing space.

The official report also records an earlier Walsh run down the channel and delivery towards Sjoeke Nüsken.

Her involvement therefore extended beyond waiting for opportunities to shoot. She helped Chelsea approach the penalty area from different positions.

There was a similar reward against Arsenal a week earlier. Walsh’s saved effort fell to Thompson for the only goal, as covered in our report of Chelsea’s derby victory.

That sequence illustrates why a dangerous shot can matter even when the goalkeeper stops it. Chelsea had a runner ready to attack the rebound.

Our assessment is that Walsh’s shooting gives defenders an additional problem. Closing her down can open a passing route; allowing her time leaves the goalkeeper exposed to a player already finding her range.

Walsh has also explained why she feels comfortable alongside Potter.

Speaking after the Arsenal victory in an interview published by Chelsea, she said:

“Lexi is really intelligent. She makes me feel really comfortable when I’m playing next to her.”

Her wider assessment emphasised collective responsibility, with team-mates covering for each other and working to make Chelsea harder to beat.

That matters when assessing midfield influence. Walsh’s freedom to help an attack depends partly on the positioning and decisions of those around her.

Potter subsequently started alongside her in Chelsea’s confirmed team against Lyon, giving their partnership another demanding European assignment.

The encouraging feature is the trust Walsh describes. Bompastor has an experienced organiser working with a younger midfielder whose reading of the game she clearly values.

BBC Sport reported that Barcelona rejected Arsenal’s bid for Walsh in September 2024.

Chelsea completed her signing in January 2025 on a four-and-a-half-year contract. Her official club profile outlines the technical quality and ability to dictate tempo that underpinned that recruitment.

Those qualities remain central to her value. Three early goals strengthen the attacking contribution around them, without establishing that she has changed position or should abandon her deeper responsibilities.

For Bompastor, the opportunity is to preserve that balance: Walsh helping Chelsea progress through midfield, supporting those around her and recognising when the space opens for a finish.

#2148. John Clark's granddaughter opens up on bid to immortalise Lisbon Lion with statue

"People knew the Lisbon Lion, but to me he was just grandad"’ says Clare Clark, articulating how it felt to be the apple of the eye of a Celtic legend.

“I still tear up every time I think or talk about him. It has been really hard since he left us last year. He was just such a big part of my life.”

John Clark passed away at the age of 84 in June last year. A surgeon in urology, his granddaughter still finds it hard to process the loss.

As a medical student at Aberdeen University, she would maintain the long-established practice of calling the old Bhoy every night at 10pm. Just to check in and get a rundown on his day.

“I actually called him Gan Gan. He was always just Gan Gan to me. So much so that when my mum or my gran talk about him even now they still refer to him as GG. It stuck.

“He was one of the driest and funniest men you could meet. He would always have a sly wee one liner that he delivered so softly it was easy to miss it altogether.”

The best story told about Clark – Luggy to friends and team-mates – dates back to the summer of 1981. Celtic were in New York to compete in the Trans-Atlantic Challenge Cup, a four team pre-season tournament also featuring the New York Cosmos, Seattle Sounders and Southampton.

In a hotel lift, winger Davie Provan was standing with the club’s assistant manager of the time when the doors opened and Pele, arguably the greatest footballer of all time, walked in.

Performing promotion duties for Escape To Victory, the Brazilian icon recognised a former opponent from a friendly international between Scotland and Brazil at Hampden prior to the 1966 World Cup.

“Scotland, Hampden, you number six – a very good player,” said Pele in warm tones. The unassuming Clark shook the great man’s hand in acknowledgement and smiled politely.

When the lift reached his floor, Pele walked away with a final wave. The doors closed and Clark turned to Provan – the winger’s jaw still pinned to the floor – and asked straight-faced, “Who the hell was that?”

A football anorak, he had an encyclopedic memory for dates, opponents and games. Wagging a finger in the air, he would recall the name of a guy he marked in 1964 as if it was yesterday. If he failed to recognise Pele, it was a first.

Marink Reedijk lays down Aberdeen challenge to Hibs team after Hearts win It pushes you forward – Jacob Murrell feeling fuelled by Falkirk fan fervour

Confirming the veracity of the story, his granddaughter tells The Fixture: “My mum always told me that lift story was true. He used to laugh about it.” Most people did, to be fair.

For the best part of six decades, Clark served Celtic as a player, coach, assistant manager, long-standing kit man and club ambassador. Willie Maley might be the only man in Celtic’s history to give more years of his life to the club.

A central defender who formed a formidable partnership with Billy McNeill, he was just five foot eight and referred to almost everyone he met as “big man”. Yet, when he passed away, it felt like the Scottish game had lost a gentle giant.

A winner of four league titles, four League Cups and three Scottish Cups, his crowning glory was Lisbon 1967 when Celtic were crowned champions of Europe.

The 60th anniversary of that Inter Milan victory falls next May, with Jim Craig, Bobby Lennox and Willie Wallace the only Lions still alive to mark the occasion. Craig spoke in these pages of his own battle with Alzheimer’s this year and all three

Lions face health challenges likely to limit their involvement in the celebrations.

For the Clark family, last year’s loss will only add to the poignancy and, in a quest to preserve his memory, the John Clark Legacy Trust has been established to raise funds for a statue in his honour.

A special charity walk is scheduled for three stages on Saturday, November 21. The first stage will cover Chapelhall to Billy McNeill’s statue in Bellshill. The second will move to Jimmy Johnstone’s plinth in Viewpark. The final leg will see those taking part walk to Celtic Park.

“Grandad would have said, ‘I don’t want people to put themselves out’.

“He would say, ‘You don’t need to do that’.

“He would have thought there were more remarkable people who deserved it more than him…”

Celtic supporters would disagree with that sentiment. Signing for the club at the age of 17, Clark’s first appearance came at Arbroath’s Gayfield in 1959 and his first goal in a Scottish Cup replay at Hibernian two years later. Between April of 1965 and September ’67 he turned out in 140 consecutive matches with no injuries, no squad rotation. If he was fit, he played.

Where other members of Jock Stein’s greatest team were natural extroverts who revelled in wine, women and song, Clark was more considered; an unassuming man with a dry sense of humour who was uncomfortable in the spotlight.

“He was always a very hands-on grandad,” adds Clare. “He went under the radar. He wouldn’t do anything to be big or flashy.

“I remember the day he unfurled the league flag at Parkhead [in 2015] and he was nervous because he didn’t know how people would react to him doing it. He was humble like that.”

A Parkhead regular from the age of three, a matchday ticket would usually come with a £10 or £20 note attached without fuss or ceremony.

“It was always just the small gestures he preferred. We high fived on Christmas Day because we were never a touchy-feely family. But that was how he was, undemonstrative.

“He was quietly there in the background if you needed anything and he was one of the kindest and most patient people. So much so that I probably had him wrapped around my finger at times.”

Clark’s own father, John senior, was a railway engineer who died in an accident in Watford when his son was just 10.

Thrust into the role of bread-winner for his mother and siblings, a local farmer allowed him to sell eggs and potatoes to make ends meet.

“During the 50th anniversary celebrations for the Lisbon Lions, the BBC did a documentary and that was the first time we as a family had really heard my grandad talk about the loss of his father,” said Clare.

“It had probably affected him more than he wanted to say. But I don’t know if he would have been the man he was without going through those hardships.

“His siblings Michael, Maureen and Margaret are still here and they idolised him because he had to stand up and do things he didn’t want to do.

“Aunt Margaret has no memory of her dad. And granddad kind of filled that role for her. He always wanted to do the best for his family.”

John Clark in action for Celtic (Image: Colorsport / Shutterstock)

Clark actually had two families in life. The first was his wife Eileen, children Martin and Marie and their three grandchildren. The other were the band of brothers famously assembled from within a 30-mile radius of Glasgow to become the first British winners of the European Cup.

There are times, now, when the current team’s performances and results in UEFA club competitions feel like an affront to the memory of the halcyon days. While Thursday’s win over Besiktas avoided a record run of five straight defeats in Europe, the 60th anniversary will serve as a stark reminder of how far standards have fallen.

“It will be hard next year for a number of reasons,” says Clare. “It probably will be the last big anniversary with any of the Lions there.

“They are getting older and there is a personal side to that. We are really close with the family of Bobby Lennox, one of the last living lions.

“When grandad was alive, he would speak to Bobby every day. During Covid they would be on the phone a couple of times a day, it was the thing that kept them going.

“You would hear grandad telling Bobby to make sure he got out and got his steps in round the garden or discussing a game on the TV.

“They were like brothers actually. And we know the McNeill family well, because we were all in Aberdeen together. And it’s sad there are not many Lions left now.

“This 60th anniversary will be very different from the 50th, that’s for sure.”

Approached by North Lanarkshire Council last year, the John Clark Legacy Trust is not the first established to raise money for a Lisbon Lions statue. It is the most recent.

Jim Goodwin says Dundee Utd are eager to keep momentum going against Motherwell Celtic's 'Che Guevaras' should show their fellow fans some respect at this year's AGM

“We want to get the statue in Chapelhall, the same way as Billy [McNeill] has one in Bellshill, Bobby [Lennox] has one in Saltcoats and Tommy [Gemmell] has one in Craigneuk,” said Clare.

The estimated cost of the memorial will be £120,000, with council grants and a series of fundraising events assisting the cause. Roughly 21 kilometres, the charity walk is expected to take five hours and any additional money raised will be put to use in the Chapelhall community.

“We want to carry on pursuing the issues that grandad valued and he really did value the people of Chapelhall,” added Clare.

“Everyone knew him even though he hadn’t lived there for 40 years.

“In this day and age, with the cost of living and putting children through football training every week, kids who are not going to be the next Celtic or Rangers stars still deserve the opportunity to play the game.

“Our plan is to work with local schools and teams to help fund their programmes to get as many young players as possible involved in the game. I think that’s what granddad would have wanted.”

For details of how to donate to the John Clark Legacy Trust email: johnclarklegacytrust@gmail.com

#2145. Arsenal transfer news: Bournemouth make Eli Junior Kroupi decision ahead of January window

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal have been dealt a potential setback in their pursuit of Bournemouth forward Eli Junior Kroupi. The Cherries are reportedly refusing to consider a January sale.

The Gunners are not alone in keeping an eye on the 20-year-old after he returned from injury. Manchester United have also made enquiries, and Tottenham remains a strong possibility after chasing him over the summer.

However, Bournemouth are keen to keep Kroupi at the Vitality Stadium following the January transfer window. His return will provide new head coach Marco Rose with another attacking threat. Helping to improve their position in the Premier League.

This could mean that Arsenal will have to plan a long-term pursuit of the player.

Kroupi provided a positive impression in his debut season in English football. Becoming one of the most exciting young forwards in the Premier League.

Overall, he hit the target on 13 occasions in the previous league campaign. Attracting the attention of a number of top European clubs.

Paris Saint Germain, Barcelona, Chelsea and Tottenham were among the clubs that were said to keep following his progress over the summer months.

However, he was unable to make the move after suffering an injury to his foot which required surgery. Kroupi has now returned to action. He came on as a late substitute in Bournemouth’s 3-0 victory against Manchester United last weekend.

Arsenal have again been linked. The Gunners are now known to be one of a number of clubs to have asked about the player’s injury and fitness ahead of improving their attacking options.

Tottenham are also interested, having had previous interest in signing him. There is no real suggestion an offer has been made.

Bournemouth are the main problem for Arsenal, with Kroupa said to be unwilling to leave the Cherries in January. He is seen as an important asset for them in the second half of the season.

However, Rose’s side has had a difficult start to the season so far. Having him at his disposal could prove to be vital. The forward can bring goals and quality into the attack. Bournemouth believes it will help change their fortunes in the coming months.

They will also look to keep their attacking unit intact. Players such as Rayan and Marcus Tavernier are ones that Bournemouth will look to keep hold of. Alex Scott may also catch the eye of other clubs ahead of the New Year.

Bournemouth will be desperate to avoid losing a number of key players in one transfer window.

That stance leaves Arsenal facing a difficult decision if they want to pursue Kroupi. The Gunners can continue monitoring his performances and fitness, but a January move currently looks unlikely.

The forward’s return to regular football will give interested clubs another opportunity to assess his progress. Arsenal, Tottenham and Manchester United could therefore remain attentive to developments as the season continues.

Bournemouth are currently in the best position and Arsenal might have to wait until the summer to see how the Cherries will hold up when faced with losing one of their most promising young players.

#2144. ‘Matter of fact’ – Virgil van Dijk issues Liverpool reality check after defeat to Arsenal

Virgil van Dijk has advised Liverpool to forget about what their Premier League rivals are doing and concentrate on themselves, following the defeat to Arsenal at the weekend.

The Reds now trail the champions and leaders by nine points after the 1-0 loss at Anfield on Sunday, and Daily Mail writer Ian Ladyman said that the result shows that Andoni Iraola’s side are ‘not ready‘ to properly contend for the top-flight title this season.

It was a second home defeat in the space of a month for LFC, who were also beaten on their own patch by second-placed Manchester City, and the veteran Dutch defender acknowledged that his team are in a transitional phase after changing head coach over the summer.

Speaking to reporters after the defeat to Arsenal, Van Dijk proclaimed (via Liverpool Echo): “We have to just focus on ourselves.

“I don’t think it is good to think about the other two [Arsenal and Man City] and even [third-placed] Brighton. We have to focus on ourselves and we are in a process.

“We have been together now for four months but we play at Liverpool and we want results every three days, especially at home.

“The main thing is we keep working very hard and that we see improvement, but the matter of fact is we are in a transition. [Arsenal] was a great opportunity to get a good result, but unfortunately it didn’t happen, so we have to take it.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Nobody realistically expected Liverpool to challenge for the title at the start of the season, so it wouldn’t be a failure if the Reds aren’t in contention for the trophy by the time April rolls around.

The objective for this campaign was to finish closer to the summit and show discernible improvement on a wretched 2025/26, and recent performances suggest that Iraola is capable of delivering in that regard.

His team are currently on course to end the season with 63 points (just three more than last term), but if they improve on a home record of just one win from five league games so far, they should be able to nudge that projected total into the 70s.

Liverpool competed admirably with Arsenal and Man City (up until the harsh red card for Ryan Gravenberch) but weren’t as clinical as the top two in those games. With more ruthlessness in attack, the Reds seem capable of at least closing that gap.

Van Dijk is correct – the goal for this season isn’t to compete for the title, but to qualify for the Champions League with relative comfort and lay the foundations for further improvement in 2027/28.

There’s still plenty of room for improvement, but at least there are signs of this campaign being much more convincing than 2025/26 had been.

#2142. Arsenal could expose Liverpool’s biggest weakness at Anfield

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal have spent years struggling to impose themselves at Anfield. However, Mikel Arteta could have an opportunity to change that record when his side face Liverpool on Sunday.

The Gunners have often struggled against the physicality of Liverpool on Merseyside. Since Jurgen Klopp took charge, Liverpool have built a reputation for being high pressing, fast, and making their opponents make mistakes.

However, looking at the latest stats, it seems that Liverpool are no longer as physical, meaning that Arsenal could target their weaknesses.

The disparity is stark between the two teams, with Liverpool having run the least distance of any Premier League side this season. Meanwhile, Arsenal are running the most.

If Arteta’s side can capitalise on this and create fast attacks from possession, then they can utilise this to their advantage.

Liverpool have shifted away from their high-octane style associated with much of Klopp’s reign. Arne Slot has slowly moved towards a more possession-based approach, focusing on keeping the ball.

Andoni Iraola looks keen to add more intensity to their pressing. The Reds have had some of the best pressing stats in the Premier League this season (Opta Analyst).

However, they are not getting the desired intensity from the system.

In addition to chasing the opposition when they have the ball, players must quickly recover, cut off passing lanes and return to their defensive positions.

This is where Arsenal might have an opportunity. If they can get past Liverpool’s first line of pressure, they may have space to push forward before the home side can get back into position.

Another area that the Gunners could target is Liverpool’s midfield.

Alexis MacAllister and Ryan Gravenberch are technically gifted and very composed when they have the ball. But neither are a true defensive midfielder, which raises questions over their ability to stop teams playing through the middle.

Arsenal have the players to exploit this. Declan Rice can put in an energetic, physical performance and drive the play forward. Myles Lewis-Skelly provides another option who can run with the ball into dangerous areas.

This can cause problems for the Liverpool midfield. Arsenal can try to disrupt Mac Allister and Gravenberch, not letting them set the tempo of the game.

If they win these individual battles, it can stop Liverpool from getting the ball control they want.

The task is certainly a tall one. Arsenal have lost their last 16 games at Anfield, including seven in Arteta’s reign.

Whilst that may be the case, the current Liverpool team are very different to the one Arsenal have faced on a regular basis during Klopp’s time in charge.

They certainly can’t expect to be outplayed, but they must take advantage of Liverpool’s failings.

Arsenal are currently second in the Premier League with 21 points from eight games. They will want to win here but will need to do more than just run them into the ground.

If Rice and Lewis-Skelly can push forward and pressurise them, forcing them to make mistakes, then Arteta could finally have his winning recipe at Anfield.

#2141. De Winter discusses clean sheet chances, relationship with Pavlovic and starting role: “I feel good”

Today, Koni De Winter sat alongside Ruben Amorim for a press conference ahead of AC Milan vs. Ferencvaros in the Europa League.

It’s been a big year for De Winter, with the Belgian going from strength to strength. After the Supercoppa Italiana game against Napoli, the defender has seemingly improved constantly,  even becoming something of a regular for Massimiliano Allegri towards the tail end of last season.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Then, Ruben Amorim has relied on him this season. So far, he’s played in every game of the campaign, and whilst the defence has been hampered by injuries around him, he’s been an ever-present option for the Portuguese.

After the birth of his child today, he was celebrated by the team at Milanello, and he will hope to make a strong week – after the derby win, too – an even better one, with a win tomorrow.

Speaking alongside Amorim, De Winter answered several questions from the media, as MilanNews relays.

This year you became a regular for AC Milan and now you’ve also become a father…

“Becoming a dad is a wonderful thing. On the pitch, I feel more important than last year and I feel more confident. I think it’s a great moment, but there’s still a long way to go.”

You’ve played pretty much all the time so far. Did you expect that?

“I feel good. When there are a lot of games, you train by playing. For me, it’s better this way. So for me, it’s fine like this; I have to be ready.”

You almost always play alongside Pavlovic. How do you get along with the Serbian?

“I feel good with all my teammates; we know each other well. I like playing with Pavlovic; he’s strong and we understand each other very well.”

How difficult will it be to keep a clean sheet tomorrow?

“They’re a good team, they’re having a good Europa League, they have great confidence, but we’ll do our best to stop them from scoring.”

Milan has had many great defenders. Do you have any role models?

“I have several. If I have to name one who played for AC Milan, I’d say Thiago Silva, whom I followed a lot and who is very good. If I have to name one, I’d say his.”

“As the coach says, he’s someone who works hard and always gives his all. Everyone loves him. When you work hard, your chance comes, and he grabbed it with both hands. We’re all happy for him.”

#2139. Arsenal news: Arsenal vs Liverpool. Arteta targets Anfield record as Liverpool prepare for Premier League clash

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal vs Liverpool: Arsenal travel to Anfield on Sunday looking to maintain their strong start to the Premier League season and end a long wait for victory at Liverpool’s home ground.

A blistering start to the season has seen Mikel Arteta’s side win seven of their first eight league games, leaving them tied on points with Manchester City at the top of the table and six points ahead of Liverpool going into the game.

However, the Gunners have not beaten Liverpool in the Premier League at Anfield since 2012/13. Arteta will be aware that the Reds are starting to gel under Andoni Iraola.

They have won three of their last four league games after a lacklustre start and had their first league win at home this season with their 2-0 win over Brighton.

After replacing Arne Slot over the summer, Iraola brought his impressive three-year spell at Bournemouth to Anfield. However, Liverpool have struggled to adjust to the new manager, losing 3 of their first 4 league games.

But they have recently improved and are looking to play a much faster style of football. As Adrian Clarke notes, they are pressing more, not sitting back as much and wanting to attack quickly when they win the ball.

They have been the most direct attackers in the Premier League. They have pace to spare within their attack, with players like Alexander Isak and Cody Gakpo providing a threat in advanced areas.

Isak comes into the game in good form after finding the back of the net six times in his first eight league games. He has scored in three successive games in the Premier League for the first time since joining the club.

Florian Wirtz will also play a key role in Liverpool’s attacking play. The German has been consistently creating chances for the team playing behind Isak in a 4-2-3-1 system. He has created nine chances against Brentford.

Arsenal will need to limit the space available to Liverpool’s forward players. Allowing Isak and Gakpo to combine freely could make an already difficult away fixture even harder.

Arsenal have had some positive news on their squad ahead of their Merseyside trip. Declan Rice and Riccardo Calafiori are both back in training.

Christos Tzolis has also trained after missing October. Noni Madueke has also not been ruled out by Arteta ahead of Sunday’s match after he missed the Fleetwood game.

They also have their own fitness issues to contend with. Iraola confirmed that Dominik Szoboszlai and Victor Munoz are both available after suffering injuries earlier in the month.

Jeramie Frimpong will be missing due to a hamstring issue but should be back after the international break. Hugo Ekitike, Geovanni Leoni and Conor Bradley are all still sidelined with longer-term injuries.

The presence of Rice will be key for Arsenal. He has been one of the best progressive midfielders in the Premier League, along with their captain, Martin Odegaard.

Despite losing just one of their last eight Premier League clashes against Liverpool, Arsenal are not as strong on the road.

The Gunners have not won any league games in their last 13 trips to Anfield. They have drawn five and lost eight. Their last win was a 2-0 victory back in the 2012/13 season.

They have also never gone 14 games unbeaten away from home in the league. That would be a great achievement.

However, there is good reason to believe they can do it. They have only conceded five clean sheets in the league this season. They have faced the least number of shots on target and the fewest expected goals.

Liverpool have also been on a strong run defensively with four clean sheets in their last six league games, so Arsenal must make their chances count.

A win would provide another key result against a direct rival, improving their position at the top of the Premier League table and finally putting an end to one of the clubs’ longest-running away day woes.

#2137. How Iceland tackled its substance abuse crisis with sport and what Scotland can learn

Abler, the software that was launched by former Icelandic handball player Markus Maute nine years ago and is now used by the majority of sporting organisations in his homeland, has made it across the North Atlantic to Scotland.

Aberdeen, Alloa Athletic, Ayr United, Dundee United, Montrose, Stenhousemuir and Stirling Albion are among the growing number of senior clubs here whose academies and community trusts have embraced the bespoke administrative system.

Grassroots outfits like Corstorphine Dynamo, Giffnock Soccer Centre, Jeanfield Swifts, North Merchiston and Vics in the Community, too, have found that many mundane but vital tasks have become far simpler since tapping into the online platform. Who knows where it will all end?

“The entire sports network over here in Iceland runs on our software and app now,” said Maute. “It has even grown outside the realm of sport. I think something like 75 per cent of people here are on Abler in some capacity. Even God is on it these days. The churches here organise their services using it as well.”

Could Scotland address some of its societal problems by following Iceland's innovative approach to youth sport as well? Maute believes there would be considerable benefits.

He is still grateful for the support which he received on a daily basis at his local handball club in Reykjavik when he was a young boy growing up in a single-parent family back in the 1980s and 1990s.

The encouragement and expert advice he was given enabled him to fulfil his childhood dream and play the game professionally when he left school; he turned out for HSG Dusseldorf in the German Bundesliga for two seasons during the early 2000s.

Yet the former left back saw first-hand just how seismic an impact increased government funding for sport had on the wider population in Iceland, not just those who, like him, excelled in their chosen pastime, during his formative years.

Alarming rates of drinking, smoking and drug use among adolescents prompted politicians at national and regional level to develop and roll out a groundbreaking prevention model which has attracted attention around the world.

Instead of investing in awareness campaigns, higher policing levels and more robust border controls, they put money into organised leisure and sport, facilitated stronger parental involvement and restricted access to alcohol and tobacco. The results have been nothing short of remarkable.

“In the late 1980s and early 1990s there was a substance abuse crisis in Iceland among teenagers,” said Maute. “In 1998, 42 per cent of Icelandic 15 and 16-year-olds reported having been drunk in the previous 30 days, 23 per cent had smoked daily and 17 per cent had used cannabis. The youth of the country was not in a good place.

A kid plays football on a pitch in Iceland (Image: Promotional)

“So the politicians came together and decided they needed to find something else for youngsters to do. They did a really good thing for once - they started to invest in sport. They built very good infrastructure for sports clubs in all of their municipalities. Suddenly, everyone had excellent, accessible facilities. That was key to the turnaround.

“Clubs here received a lot of financial support. The government built the infrastructure and then gifted the facilities to them so they didn't have to fund the construction of a new pitch, changing rooms, or a stand. Those things can be a huge burden on clubs.

“The policy worked. Clubs became like second homes for kids. We hung out there before practice and after practice when I was growing up. I was lucky enough to have great coaches who taught me good values and life skills through sport as well as how to play handball and football.

“A lot of money was put into coach education as well. There are not that many professional coaches in Iceland, but they do get compensated for their time. There are also full-time staff who run clubs now. The set-ups are far more professional and everything is subsidised.

“The municipalities also give parents financial incentives to get their kids involved. For example, I get around £500 a year to use towards my son playing sport or getting involved in music. The money I receive is substantial and definitely makes a big difference.

“It costs around £800 or £900 a year for my 12-year-old boy to play football. But he trains four times a week with a UEFA-qualified coach and he plays games on top of that. It is definitely good value for money.”

Since reaching the quarter-finals of Euro 2016 and qualifying for Russia 2018 – incredible achievements for a country which had a population of just 335,000 people at that time – the Iceland national football team has struggled.

They have many excellent players who ply their trade in the Bundesliga, Eredivisie, La Liga, Ligue 1, Premier League and Serie A. But they failed to reach the Euro 2020, Qatar 2022, Euro 2024 and World Cup 2026 finals.

The focus in the youth game, though, is very much on participation.

“The whole sport system is built around community clubs which are accessible to everyone,” said Maute. “The beauty of it is that it is open to all. Yes, it develops all our best athletes and all of our top footballers. But everyone comes through the same grassroots system.

“Any kid can play. If you have a boy who has never played football and your area has the best football team in the country at his age group then he will still be welcomed with open arms and he will still train alongside the best players.

“People in Iceland understand the important impact this has had in preventing anti-social behaviour. In my neighbourhood, something like 90 per cent of kids who are aged between six and 16 participate in organised sports at their local club. The substance abuse problem which was once so bad has changed enormously.”

Indeed it has. Official figures show that between 1998 and 2018 the proportion of 15 and 16-year-olds in Iceland who reported having been drunk in the previous 30 days fell from 42 per cent to five per cent, daily smoking fell from 23 per cent to three per cent and cannabis use fell from 17 per cent to five per cent.

Kids play football at a community club in Iceland (Image: Promotional)

For Maute and many of his compatriots, the millions which have been pumped into sport have been extremely well spent.

“When kids play sport they get integrated into society,” he said. “They are more likely to get a better job, pay higher taxes, use the healthcare system less and rely on income support and benefits less.”

Maute has been championing the merits of the Abler software to sports clubs, youth organisations, charities and governing bodies across Europe for some time now and has been struck by how differently other countries, including Scotland, deal with their young people and tackle alcohol and drug abuse issues.

“What I like most about the Icelandic system is that nobody has to do trials in order to get into a team,” he said. “That is a very good thing. Even if you’re not an elite-level player, you can still take part. So kids who maybe develop a little slower physically, who might be late bloomers, are not discriminated against.

“I would say that 90 per cent of the boys who are in my son’s class at school take part in his football and handball training. You remain in your friendship circle even if you are an elite guy. Don’t get me wrong, it is still a serious environment where a good player can progress. But you form connections which last a lifetime.

“The community clubs are also in close proximity to the schools. In other countries it can sometimes take a kid an hour to get to training. If they are any good, they are invariably whisked away to another club to play with other promising players and are taken out of their friendship group.

“Putting kids into a professional environment too early is the worst thing you can do in my opinion. Studies have been done which show that if sport becomes too serious too early it puts kids at risk of burnout. Their interest in sport can often fizzle out when they are as young as 12 if they are taken away by an academy.”

The Scottish government continues to wrestle with significant public-health challenges which affect both young people and the wider population.

Concerning Public Health Scotland figures which were released in December showed that 24 per cent of Primary One children were at risk of being overweight or obese in the 2024/25 school year.

The same body revealed in January that there were 29,430 hospital admissions for “conditions wholly attributable to alcohol” during the 2024/25 financial year. National Records of Scotland confirmed that 1,133 drug use deaths were registered here in 2025.

It is overly simplistic to suggest these complex issues can be tackled by allocating increased funding to youth football teams. Still, MSPs at Holyrood and MPs at Westminster could learn much if they studied the success which Iceland has enjoyed through its internationally renowned prevention model.

“I don’t want to comment on how Scottish people should run their country,” said Maute. “But what I would say is that I believe in the Icelandic sports model and the Icelandic methodology. Look at what it has done to our substance abuse issues. The evidence is there.

Markus Maute of Abler (Image: Promottional)

“I have lived it myself. My upbringing would have been completely different without my local sports club. It made me what I am today and it has done the same for so many other kids.

“The government has had to invest for it to work. The infrastructure has been expensive. Good coaches have to be able to make a living out of sport or at least be paid. You can’t depend on volunteers. But when they put money in they know they can get it back later many times over. The numbers prove it.

“More often than not individuals who come through the system will get a job, will pay taxes, will know how to take care of themselves, will understand how to bounce back from adversity when things are not going so well in life.

“I am a great believer in this. I feel we're only scratching the surface of how the life-developing power of sport can be harnessed too. I think it will become even more important in future because so much communication is digital now and sport is one of the last places where kids can come together and connect.”

He added, “I grew up without a father and was very fortunate to have good sports clubs in my neighbourhood during my childhood. I spent most of my time in and around them. The coaches there became my father figures. They gave me a sense of belonging.

“As my business career progressed, I developed ideas about how to improve sport further in Iceland. I said, ‘Do you know what? I'm going to go and talk about my ideas to all the people I respect who are involved in the system because I believe in it a lot.’ They thought my ideas were great so I quit my job and went all in.

“I thought we needed to amplify the life-developing power of sports. Sport is such a powerful tool because kids are so emotionally invested in it. They listen more to their coach than they do their teacher.

“Abler helps to create a better working environment for everyone who is involved in sport so more time and more energy can be put into what really matters: developing good people and great athletes.”

#2136. Arsenal injury news: Arteta faces selection dilemma ahead of Liverpool clash

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal will have a few key injury updates ahead of their clash with Liverpool on Sunday, but Mikel Arteta still has some tough choices to make in his front line.

Declan Rice, Riccardo Calafiori and Christos Tzolis have returned to training so there is hope they can play in the Premier League clash against Anfield.

Bukayo Saka is still out with a quad injury, but Noni Madueke looks to be fit to go into the final game. Max Dowman is also suspended after Arsenal were unable to overturn his red card suspension after the defeat to Fleetwood in the Carabao Cup.

However, Arteta has some good options to choose from. He can ill afford any more injuries in attack.

Rice’s return will be key after missing the midweek cup clash.

The midfielder was subbed in the win against Everton with a knock. His absence was a blow against Fleetwood.

He trained on Friday. As long as he does another session on Saturday, he will be available. Rice is a big boost for Arsenal in the middle of the park as they face a Liverpool side who have been in good form recently.

Calafiori is also edging closer to a return. The Italian has played in the last seven Premier League games for Arsenal but missed against Everton.

However, he was only on for a short period when he came on as a sub. But he seemed to have an issue. Arteta said that it didn’t seem like a big problem, but they’re taking some caution.

Like Rice, Calafiori trained on Friday and will be able to go to Merseyside if he finishes his session on Saturday.

Tzolis is another possible addition. The winger has missed Arsenal’s last five matches after suffering a hamstring injury while playing for Greece in October.

His latest scan showed encouraging progress, and he has now returned to training. A further successful session would put him in contention for Sunday’s match.

The main issue for Arteta will be his lack of attacking width.

Madueke did not join the squad for the trip to Fleetwood due to an ankle injury sustained in the Everton game. He did not train on Monday, but Arteta has not ruled him out of the game against Liverpool. He will have to finish his session on Saturday.

Dowman is also missing due to suspension. He was sent off in the 1-1 draw against Fleetwood after a challenge from Harrison Neal. His appeal was rejected by the FA, so he will miss the game against Liverpool.

Saka makes it even worse for the Gunners. The England international has a quad injury from the Bayern Munich game and is likely to be out for several weeks.

Arteta confirmed that he will need another scan before there is a clearer timeline for his return but is likely to be out for a long period of time.

William Saliba is still missing from the squad, having aggravated a back injury during his time playing for France at the World Cup this summer.

But the defender has returned to doing one-on-one training on the training ground.

Arsenal will perform an MRI scan to monitor his progress. Arteta has suggested there have been positive signs, and he is expected back in December.

The main talking point will be Rice, Calafiori and Tzolis returning to training ahead of Sunday.

It would give Arsenal a nice boost with all three players fit. Madueke is still a doubt and they have fewer options going forward with Saka and Dowman out.

#2135. Willian admits Chelsea exit was a ‘big mistake’ after wanting Arsenal escape just THREE months later

Graduate Sports Journalist with a love for football.

Willian has described leaving Chelsea for Arsenal as a mistake, explaining how quickly his enthusiasm for the move turned into regret.

Speaking to former team-mate John Obi Mikel on The Obi One Podcast, the Brazilian revisited the decision that ended his seven-year Stamford Bridge spell in 2020.

Chelsea offered two more years, while Willian wanted three. His contract-length disagreement with Chelsea featured in ReadChelsea’s coverage at the time.

Arsenal offered the longer deal. Willian also valued keeping his family in London and says Mikel Arteta outlined a central role for him.

“To be honest I should have stayed,” he said, in comments reported by the Mirror.

The winger followed the same route as Petr Cech, who explained his own Chelsea-to-Arsenal move through the desire for regular football.

Willian says the reality differed from the picture presented during talks. Within three months, he had told his agent he wanted to leave.

He linked that frustration to his performances, explaining that unhappiness prevented him from producing his best football.

His Arsenal spell ultimately lasted one season. He scored once in 37 appearances before leaving for Corinthians in 2021.

#2134. Arsenal transfer news: Gunners target Brazilian teenager Eduardo Pape

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal are increasing their interest in Cruzeiro youngster Eduardo Pape.

Despite the fact he has yet to play for the senior side in Brazil, the 17-year-old has attracted interest from a number of clubs. It is understood that Arsenal is keeping an eye on the youngster. Newcastle United and Bayern Munich are also linked with the midfielder.

The move could be a good fit with the Gunners liking to identify young talent early on. However, Pape is still very much in the early stages of his career, and any move would be seen as an investment into his future.

Arsenal are reportedly targeting Pape as a potential signing in 2027. The teenager is valued at around £33m.

Pape has already had previous experience playing for Cruzeiro’s Under-20s. He’s played 32 times and scored three goals and three assists.

He has left a successful headline in junior football as well. Pape has netted six goals in eight matches and strives for a place in the Brazilian U17 World Cup squad.

The tournament gives an opportunity to demonstrate his skills. The scouts will closely monitor the tournament, so any team interested in the player will be able to see his progress.

Of course, not just Arsenal would want to sign him. Clubs like Newcastle and Bayern Munich have already expressed interest in securing him. This is likely to cause a competitive atmosphere.

There is a concern that there hasn’t been any senior-level play from Pape. As he has never played first-team football before. It will be difficult to see how he will adapt to playing in Europe.

The contract is until December 2029, meaning Cruzeiro are in a strong position. There isn’t a huge need for the Brazilian club to rush into any decisions, meaning Pape can get some experience in a comfortable environment before moving abroad.

However, they have been very successful in the past at bringing in young Brazilian players. Gabriel Martinelli was brought in from Ituano as a teenager and has become an important first team player.

It gives us an insight into what Pape could hope to achieve.

It shows that Arsenal are looking further ahead with their recruitment. Whether they make a move for him remains to be seen. Pape will be one to keep an eye on as we approach the 2027 transfer window.

#2133. Chelsea Women face World Cup play-off tests as Lexi Potter targets first senior England appearance

Graduate Sports Journalist with a love for football.

Chelsea Women’s October internationals bring World Cup play-offs, a possible England debut for Lexi Potter and meetings between club team-mates.

The senior schedule runs from 9 to 14 October, before Sonia Bompastor’s side resume their Women’s Super League campaign against Tottenham.

Here are the call-ups, fixtures and UK kick-off times supporters need.

Last updated: 6 October 2026. All dates and times below are British Summer Time.

Potter has two opportunities to earn her first senior cap: away to Greece on Friday 9 October and in the return at Leicester’s King Power Stadium on Tuesday 13 October.

The midfielder joins Hannah Hampton, Lucy Bronze, Keira Walsh and Lauren James following her first England senior call-up.

Selection gives Potter the opportunity to make another significant step, although Sarina Wiegman has yet to confirm who will play.

England’s first leg kicks off at 17:30, with the home game starting at 19:30.

The FA’s broadcast guide confirms both matches will be shown live on ITV.

Seven countries featuring Chelsea players enter the first round of Europe’s qualifying play-offs.

Each team listed below plays its first leg away on Friday 9 October, followed by the home return on Tuesday 13 October. Italy and Switzerland’s away fixtures take place at neutral venues.

The Netherlands’ home kick-off follows the Dutch federation’s fixture listing, which gives 20:45 local time, or 19:45 BST.

No. October’s winners advance to a second two-legged tie later in the year.

According to UEFA’s play-off draw and format, England would face Slovakia or Ukraine if they overcome Greece.

Scotland and Sweden could meet in round two, potentially putting Cuthbert and Bjorn on opposite sides of a decisive tie.

Seven of the eight second-round winners qualify directly for Brazil 2027, based on the qualifying league rankings. The remaining winner enters the inter-confederation play-offs.

Chelsea’s official international squad guide also confirms the following selections.

The midnight fixtures start at the beginning of the dates shown. Canada’s first game therefore falls on Friday night into Saturday for UK viewers; the USA’s second falls on Tuesday night into Wednesday.

Girma initially featured in the USA selection but has since withdrawn.

Bompastor previously explained that the defender suffered a training knock and could miss several weeks. Our Naomi Girma injury update covers that assessment.

Thompson remains selected for Emma Hayes’ two matches against Spain.

Chelsea host Tottenham at Stamford Bridge on Sunday 18 October, kicking off at noon.

The club’s confirmed WSL television schedule lists Sky Sports as the broadcaster.

#2132. MN: Why Fonseca’s shock exit could bring forward Athekame’s Milan return

Paulo Fonseca is leaving Lyon and it could have a knock-on effect for AC Milan in the winter mercato, it seems.

With Athekame’s rise at Lyon, there are several people to thank. However, Fonseca deserves substantial praise for getting the most out of the young Swiss defender. Indeed, he always showed signs of promise and now, it’s tangible.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The Portuguese gave him a starting role in his defence, and he hasn’t looked back, playing Athekame nine times in Ligue 1 this season. However, things could change now.

According to MilanNews, Fonseca’s departure – headed to Sporting – could ‘alter the balance’ at the Groupama Stadium and offer an opportunity for the Rossoneri to recall their player. After all, he was heavily against his departure in mid-season.

For Athekame, the prospect of working under a new coach may not be as rewarding, too. So, he may also prefer a return to the Diavolo, where a role is ready for him on the right of Ruben Amorim’s back four.

There is no recall clause in the deal, so it won’t be easy and will likely require some financial outlay. Nevertheless, Fonseca may have helped Milan here, even if it’s only indirectly. More about his situation should be learned over the next few weeks.

#2131. Arsenal transfer news: Gunners explore loan move for Real Madrid striker Endrick

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal are keeping their options open regarding a January move for Real Madrid striker Endrick, as Mikel Arteta looks to strengthen his attacking options.

The Gunners have been in contact about a loan move for the Brazilian. There is also talk of a permanent move. However, Real Madrid are reluctant to let him leave and feel he has a big future ahead at the club.

Endrick has struggled to earn consistent game time at the Bernabeu.

While he is only 20 years old and has plenty of time left to make his mark at the top level, a new transfer away from Madrid could give him a more consistent role.

Arsenal are among the clubs keeping an eye on Endrick ahead of the January transfer window, reports TEAMtalk.

The forward was loaned to Lyon for the second half of last season to get more regular game time.

However, his representatives are said to be open to looking for another move if things do not improve in Madrid.

It seems Arsenal are interested in signing Endrick on loan with a view to making the move permanent. This will give them time to see how he settles into the Premier League.

However, Real Madrid may be in a strong position. They are still loyal to the young player and would prefer to keep him at the Spanish club.

Arsenal will rely on Madrid being more willing to let him go.

The reported interest from Arsenal comes as Arteta looks to strengthen his attacking options.

Endrick would provide variety to an attacking line-up which has been under pressure of late. His age and potential make a loan deal with an option to make the move permanent an attractive option.

There is also positivity from his previous stint with Lyon. He has already been on a loan move from Madrid and has demonstrated that he can make progress when getting regular game time.

Arsenal have previously signed young talents from Real Madrid.

Martin Odegaard came to north London as a young player after failing to make an impact in the first team at Madrid. He then became a key player for Arteta, taking on the role of captain.

Endrick will be hoping to follow a similar trajectory.

There is no suggestion that any deal is near despite Arsenal’s interest.

Endrick remains an important part of Real Madrid’s long-term plans. His representatives are thought to be looking at other options to help his career develop.

This loan deal would provide a solution for all parties. Arsenal would have another attacker to bring in, Endrick would play more regularly and Madrid would still have an interest in his future.

It will also depend on how willing the Spanish giants are to move their stance before January.

For now, Arsenal are in contact with Endrick as they consider a move. The Brazilian’s future may soon become a topic of interest ahead of the winter transfer window.

Geovany Quenda says he must keep working for his Chelsea opportunity after playing just 27 Premier League minutes under Xabi Alonso.

The 19-year-old addressed his limited involvement while away with Portugal Under-21s, acknowledging the competition he faces at Stamford Bridge.

Chelsea’s official player profile records two league appearances this season, both as a substitute.

In comments reported by Portuguese news agency Lusa, translated from Portuguese, he said:

“Those are the manager’s decisions. I just have to keep giving my best.”

Quenda also acknowledged the quality of his Chelsea team-mates as he attempts to secure more regular football.

The winger joined Chelsea’s early pre-season preparations under Alonso after two seasons in Sporting’s first team. His Chelsea contract runs until 2034.

For Quenda, the immediate target is straightforward: turn substitute appearances into a first Premier League start. That selection decision remains with Alonso.

#2129. Arsenal news: Arteta’s side secure landmark victory at Anfield

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

In Arsenal news, Arsenal have finally found a way to win at Anfield again. Their latest victory owed as much to defensive discipline as Kai Havertz’s decisive goal.

The Gunners claimed a 1-0 Premier League victory against Liverpool, ending their 14-year wait for victory at the iconic stadium. Declan Rice set up their opener as Havertz put the visitors ahead on the 16th minute.

It was a tough afternoon for Mikel Arteta’s side as they were put under sustained pressure by their opponents. The Reds also created a number of dangerous chances throughout the match, especially in the first half.

Arsenal held their composure, and the backline was calm throughout. David Raya made the crucial interventions to keep the lead safe.

They are now three points ahead at the top of the Premier League after Manchester City lost to Brighton.

While the attack has been a key aspect of Arsenal’s title charge, they were put under pressure defensively at Anfield.

Liverpool came out aggressively after going behind. Virgil van Dijk caused problems from set pieces, and Florian Wirtz hit the crossbar from a free kick.

Raya was quick to block Van Dijk’s dangerous flick on and prevent Alexander Isak from capitalising. However, Alexis Mac Allister did head over the bar as Liverpool looked to level the game.

Arsenal were forced into another stretch as Jurrien Timber blocked well. William Saliba and Gabriel helped to limit Liverpool’s chances.

The stats tell the story of how well the visitors defended. Liverpool had 17 shots but couldn’t get one on target.

Liverpool were unable to create a shot on target for the first time in recorded history (since 2003-04) in a Premier League game at Anfield.

Meanwhile, it was the first time that Arsenal had kept a clean sheet in the Premier League at the ground since September 2012. The result was especially important following their Carabao Cup heartbreak against Fleetwood Town earlier in the week.

Whilst they have made a great defensive stand, Arsenal’s two most influential players in the final third made the difference.

Rice won the turnover, drove down the right and played a low cross into the six-yard box. Havertz made his run to the back post and finished to give Arsenal the early lead.

The goal also takes Havertz to 46 Premier League goals, making him the most prolific German in the competition and overtaking former Manchester City midfielder Ilkay Gündogan.

Rice had a huge influence on the game. His performance in the middle of the park helped Arsenal deal with the intensity of Liverpool, and he was named man of the match.

Christos Tzolis provided an important attacking outlet. His pace created chances to ease pressure on Arsenal. He forced an incredible save from Alisson with a late first-time effort.

The win was Arsenal’s first at Anfield in any competition since September 2012 and their 300th away win in the Premier League.

Arteta’s team have had a stellar start to the season, taking 24 points from nine games. They have also proved they are capable of getting results in tough circumstances.

They will now look ahead to their Champions League game on Wednesday against Slavia Prague and then back to domestic action against Hull City.
